=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a simple connected undirected graph with N vertices and M edges, where vertices are numbered 1 to N and edges are numbered 1 to M. Edge i (1 \leq i \leq M) connects vertices u_i and v_i bidirectionally and has weight w_i.
For a path, define its weight as the maximum weight of an edge in the path.
Define f(x, y) as the minimum possible path weight of a path from vertex x to vertex y.
You are given two sequences of length K: (A_1, A_2, \ldots, A_K) and (B_1, B_2, \ldots, B_K). It is guaranteed that A_i \neq B_j (1 \leq i,j \leq K).
Permute the sequence B freely so that \displaystyle \sum_{i=1}^{K} f(A_i, B_i) is minimized.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N M K
u_1 v_1 w_1
u_2 v_2 w_2
\vdots
u_M v_M w_M
A_1 A_2 \ldots A_K
B_1 B_2 \ldots B_K

Output

Print the minimum value of \displaystyle \sum_{i=1}^{K} f(A_i, B_i).

Constraints


- 2 \leq N  \leq 2 \times 10^5
- N-1 \leq M  \leq \min(\frac{N \times (N-1)}{2},2 \times 10^5)
- 1 \leq K \leq N
- 1 \leq u_i<v_i \leq N (1 \leq i \leq M) 
- 1 \leq w_i \leq 10^9
- 1 \leq A_i,B_i \leq N (1 \leq i \leq K)
- The given graph is simple and connected.
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

4 4 3
1 3 2
3 4 1
2 4 5
1 4 4
1 1 3
4 4 2

Sample Output 1

8

If we rearrange B as (2,4,4):

- f(1,2) = 5: The path from vertex 1 to vertex 2 passing through vertex 4 contains edge 3 with a maximum edge weight of 5. There is no path with a maximum edge weight less than or equal to 4, so 5 is the minimum possible.
- f(1,4) = 2: The path from vertex 1 to vertex 4 passing through vertex 3 contains edge 1 with a maximum edge weight of 2. There is no path with a maximum edge weight less than or equal to 1, so 2 is the minimum possible.
- f(3,4) = 1: The path from vertex 3 to vertex 4 passing through the direct edge contains an edge with a maximum edge weight of 1. No path can have a maximum weight 0 or less, so 1 is the minimum possible.

Thus, \displaystyle \sum_{i=1}^{3} f(A_i, B_i) = 5+2+1=8. No permutation of B yields 7 or less, so the answer is 8.

Sample Input 2

3 3 2
1 2 5
2 3 2
1 3 1
1 1
2 3

Sample Output 2

3

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

N M K

Read all ints:
import sys
data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
it = iter(data)
N = next(it); M = next(it); K = next(it)
edges = []
for _ in range(M):
    u=next(it); v=next(it); w=next(it)
    edges.append((w,u,v))
edges.sort()
A = [next(it) for _ in range(K)]
B = [next(it) for _ in range(K)]
Need countA, countB arrays length N+1. We can count as reading A/B. But data iterator after edges. We can do:
cnt = [0]*(N+1)
for i in range(K):
    cnt[A[i]] += 1
for i in range(K):
    cnt[B[i]] -= 1
Because no both positive but net surplus. This gives leaf surplus S = countA - countB. If B count at v, cnt[v] negative. Good. We don't need separate counts. If there were both, cnt would net; but guarantee no both. If both but equal? Actually if A_i != B_j all i,j, no both. So fine.

Need initialize surplus array for tree nodes? We can store surplus for leaves and internal nodes in array `bal` length total_nodes+1. Initially bal[v] = cnt[v] for v=1..N. For internal nodes, computed. But we can compute on fly and store in bal[idx] as process. Need left, right, wt arrays.

Total nodes = 2*N (since 1-index, max 2N-1). Allocate size 2*N maybe +2. left = [0]*(2*N) ; right = [0]*(2*N); wt = [0]*(2*N); bal = [0]*(2*N). Since N up to 2e5, 2*N=4e5, fine.

DSU:
parent = list(range(N+1))
size = [1]*(N+1)
root_node = list(range(N+1))
def find(x):
    while parent[x] != x:
        parent[x] = parent[parent[x]]
        x = parent[x]
    return x

node_cnt = N
for w,u,v in edges:
    ru = find(u); rv = find(v)
    if ru == rv: continue
    node_cnt += 1
    left[node_cnt] = root_node[ru]
    right[node_cnt] = root_node[rv]
    wt[node_cnt] = w
    # union by size
    if size[ru] < size[rv]:
        ru, rv = rv, ru
    parent[rv] = ru
    size[ru] += size[rv]
    root_node[ru] = node_cnt
After loop, node_cnt should 2N-1. If graph connected. Need maybe root_node[find(1)] = node_cnt.

Now compute answer:
ans = 0
for idx in range(N+1, node_cnt+1):
    l = left[idx]; r = right[idx]
    sl = bal[l]; sr = bal[r]
    # signs opposite: sl*sr < 0
    if sl < 0 and sr > 0 or sl > 0 and sr < 0:
        if sl < 0:
            # sl negative, sr positive
            m = min(-sl, sr)
        else:
            m = min(sl, -sr)
        ans += m * wt[idx]
        # combined surplus
        bal[idx] = sl + sr
    else:
        bal[idx] = sl + sr
print(ans)

Need be careful with input guarantee "A_i != B_j (1 <= i,j <= K)" means for all i,j, A_i != B_j. So no vertex appears in both sequences. Now, is the greedy formula definitely correct for all ultrametrics? Let's attempt formal proof and edge cases.

We have a rooted binary tree with leaves, internal node weight w increasing along root? In Kruskal tree, weights are nondecreasing from leaves to root? Since edges sorted by weight, when merging, parent weight >= child weight? Actually if equal, parent weight = child weight. Nondecreasing. f(a,b)=weight of LCA. Need min matching between multiset A and B. We can define for each node u, after optimally matching within subtree u, the only possible unmatched items are all of one type (A or B), and the minimal cost inside u for matching min(A_u,B_u) pairs plus maybe surplus? But our recursive algorithm matches greedily at each internal node. Need prove optimality.

At node u with children x,y. Let S_x = A_x - B_x, S_y = A_y - B_y. Suppose S_x >0, S_y <0. The algorithm matches m=min(S_x,-S_y) A from x to B from y at cost w_u. Is it always possible to match exactly m pairs across at cost w_u? Yes any A leaf in x and B leaf in y have LCA u, cost w_u. Then remaining surplus S_x+S_y. But could there be a better matching that matches fewer across at u and instead matches those A/B with items outside u at ancestor cost > w_u? Since total counts in whole tree balanced, if we leave surplus from u to ancestor, they must eventually match outside at cost >= w_u (actually > or = if equal weights). Matching at u is no more expensive. But wait if we match A from x to B from y at u, we reduce surplus by both. If instead we don't match them, then at ancestor we might match A from x to some B outside, and B from y to some A outside. Both costs >= w_u. So matching at u is at least as good. Thus maximize cross matches at u? What about if S_x >0 and S_y >0 (same sign), algorithm doesn't match across. Could matching A from x to B from y? There is no B in y, only A surplus, so impossible. Could there be B in y that was matched internally but we could unmatch to create B for cross? Suppose y has both A and B counts, after internal matching surplus A. If we unmatch some internal pair in y, we get one A and one B in y. Then we could match the B from y with A from x at cost w_u, and the A from y with maybe B outside at ancestor. But the original internal pair cost <= w_child < w_u. Replacing it with cross at w_u and outside at ancestor likely worse. Need prove. The algorithm's assumption "maximize internal matches" is valid because any internal match cost < w_u. Let's construct potential counterexample: x has surplus A (after max internal), y has surplus A but also internal matched pairs. Maybe matching A_x with B_y (unmatched from internal) at cost w_u and A_y with B_out at ancestor cost w_anc could be better than matching A_y with B_y internally at cost c<w_u and A_x with B_out at w_anc? Compare:
Option internal: A_y-B_y cost c, A_x-B_out cost W (>=w_u).
Option cross: A_x-B_y cost w_u, A_y-B_out cost W.
Difference cross - internal = w_u - c >0, so worse. If B_out cost maybe equal w_u? Still w_u - c >0. So no. Similarly if x has B surplus. Thus maximizing internal matches is optimal.

But what if y has surplus A but also internal matches with cost equal to w_u? Is child internal node weight could equal w_u if same weight edges. Then c = w_u, difference zero. Then matching across vs internal tie. Algorithm matching internally and leaving surplus to ancestor may not be worse. But could it affect ability to match at current node with opposite surplus? Suppose x S_x >0, y S_y >0, no opposite. If we unmatch inside y to create B, we could match with x at cost w_u, and leave A_y to ancestor. Internal cost c = w_u (same weight) so tie. But algorithm doesn't add cost at u; it leaves both surpluses to ancestor. At ancestor, x surplus and y surplus same sign, so they won't match each other; they will match outside at cost >= w_u. The alternative: match x A with y B at w_u, and y A with outside at W. Total for these two = w_u + W. Algorithm: x A with outside at W, y A with outside at W? Wait y B? Let's set counts.

Need examine equal weights carefully because internal match cost can equal current node weight. Our proof "internal match < w_u" may fail if child root weight = w_u. But if child root weight = w_u, then the child component was formed by an edge of same weight. Any pair across different subcomponents within child has cost w_u too. Matching within child at cost w_u is equal to matching across at u. So greedy maximizing internal matches still optimal (ties). But need ensure formula min(|S_x|,|S_y|) when signs opposite remains correct. If signs same, no cross. Could there be benefit to unmatch internal pairs to create opposite surplus and match at current node with same cost, reducing number of items passed to ancestor? Since costs equal, no decrease. But maybe it could reduce ancestor cost? Let's test.

Consider root with two children u and v. u has surplus A=1, but also an internal pair A-B cost w (same as root). v has surplus A=1. Total A surplus 2, B? Wait total counts must balance globally; if no B outside, impossible. Need include outside B at ancestor? Root is top, total balance. Let's construct tree: root weight 10. Left child L weight 10? Actually child root weight can equal root. Suppose root has child L (internal weight 10) and leaf R. L has leaves a,b connected by weight10, plus? Let's assign counts: L has A at a, B at b, and A at c? Need L surplus maybe A? Let's define L has leaves a(A), b(B), c(A) => total A=2,B=1 surplus A=1. Internal L cost for matching a-b =10. R has B=1. Total A=2,B=2. Algorithm:
At L: a(A), b(B) opposite, cross cost10, S_L=+1 (c A). At root: S_L=+1, R(B) -1, cross cost10. Total20.
Alternative: At L, do not match a-b. Then L has A a, B b, A c. At root, we can match a-R? cost10, c-R? only one R B, b? Need total B=1 (R) plus b B=1. Match c-R cost10, a-b cost? LCA of a,b is L weight10. Total20. Same.
If L internal weight 5 < root 10, algorithm better.

What about equal weights and signs opposite at node: Suppose child x S_x=+1, child y S_y=-1. Algorithm matches at cost w. Could there be alternative to not match at u and match both at ancestor with cost w (equal) if ancestor weight same? No worse. So okay.

But there is another subtlety: The cost f(a,b) is weight of LCA. In Kruskal tree with equal weights, parent weight can equal child weight. Then the "ultrametric" property still holds but not strictly. The greedy still works with nondecreasing weights.

Let's attempt to prove via induction using "minimum cost for subtree given external demand". At node u, after optimally matching within u, the unmatched surplus is S_u = A_u - B_u. The cost incurred inside u for the pairs matched within u is minimal. But is the number of pairs matched within u always min(A_u,B_u)? Yes because any matching within u can match at most min(A_u,B_u) pairs. It is always possible to match min(A_u,B_u) pairs within u? Is it always possible to pair all minority leaves in u with majority leaves in u with LCA within u? Since u contains both A and B leaves; any A leaf and B leaf in u have LCA within u (could be u itself if in different children). We can match them, cost <= w_u. So yes. The algorithm matches as many as possible within each child and then at u, but does it necessarily match min(A_u,B_u) total within u? Let's check. At u, after children, if S_x and S_y opposite, it matches some cross; total matched within u = matched in x + matched in y + cross. Is this equal to min(A_u,B_u)? Suppose S_x=+5, S_y=-3. Child x matched min(A_x,B_x), child y matched min(A_y,B_y), cross3. Total matched = (A_x+B_x - |S_x|)/2? Let's compute A_u = A_x+A_y, B_u=B_x+B_y. Since S_x=5 means A_x-B_x=5. S_y=-3 means A_y-B_y=-3. Total A_u-B_u=2, so min(A_u,B_u)=B_u? Not necessarily. Let's derive matched count = min(A_x,B_x)+min(A_y,B_y)+3. Is this equal min(A_u,B_u)? Use identity min(a,b) = (a+b - |a-b|)/2. For x: (A_x+B_x -5)/2. y: (A_y+B_y -3)/2. +3 = (A_x+B_x+A_y+B_y -8 +6)/2 = (A_u+B_u -2)/2. Since total surplus S=2, min(A_u,B_u) = (A_u+B_u - |S|)/2 = (A_u+B_u -2)/2. Yes. If signs same S_x=5,S_y=3, cross0, matched = (A_x+B_x-5 + A_y+B_y-3)/2 = (A_u+B_u -8)/2. Total surplus S=8, min=(A_u+B_u-8)/2. So algorithm matches min(A_u,B_u) within u. Thus it maximizes internal matches. Good.

Now, if algorithm matches min(A_u,B_u) pairs within u, is the cost minimal among all matchings that match that many pairs within u? Need induction plus cross matching. At u, the only pairs whose LCA is u are cross pairs between children. All other pairs have LCA in child and cost handled by child. For a matching that matches M = min(A_u,B_u) pairs within u, let c be number of cross pairs at u. Then number of pairs within children = M - c. The cost = cost_children + c*w_u. For a fixed c, to minimize cost_children, we should maximize matches within children? But the counts available for child-internal matches depend on c. We need choose c and internal matchings. Our greedy chooses c = min(|S_x|,|S_y|) if signs opposite else 0, where S_x,S_y are surpluses after maximal internal matching in children. Is that optimal? Let's formulate.

Let child x have A_x,B_x; child y A_y,B_y. A matching at u can have c cross pairs (A from x to B from y plus maybe A from y to B from x? Wait cross pairs can be in both directions: A_x-B_y and A_y-B_x. Our greedy only matches when signs opposite: if S_x>0 (A surplus in x) and S_y<0 (B surplus in y), it matches A_x-B_y. If S_x<0, S_y>0, matches A_y-B_x. But could optimal include both directions simultaneously? That would mean some A_x-B_y and some A_y-B_x. Is that ever beneficial? Let's see. If both children have both A and B, after internal matching each has surplus of one type. Matching both directions would require unmatching internal pairs. Since internal costs <= w_u, likely not beneficial. But maybe if child internal costs equal w_u, tie. Greedy avoids both directions. Need prove no need for bidirectional cross at u.

Consider child x has A_x=5,B_x=3 (S_x=2), child y has A_y=3,B_y=5 (S_y=-2). Greedy: internal x matches 3 cost, internal y matches 3 cost, cross c=2 A_x-B_y cost 2w. Total matched = 3+3+2=8 = min(A_u=8,B_u=8). Alternative cross both directions: c1=1 A_x-B_y, c2=1 A_y-B_x, and internal matches? Let's compute counts remaining: x after c1 A_x-B_y: A=4,B=3, can internal 3; y after c2 A_y-B_x: A=2,B=4, internal 2. Total internal 5, cross2, total7? Need total matched should 8, maybe cross c total? Let's set c total=2, one each direction. Remaining x A=4,B=3 internal3; y A=2,B=4 internal2; total matched=1+1+3+2=7, but total pairs min=8. Need another cross or internal? x after internal3 leaves A=1; y after internal2 leaves B=2. Need match remaining A_x-B_y cross1. So total cross3 (2 A_x-B_y,1 A_y-B_x), internal5, total8. Cost = internal5 + 3w. Greedy cost = internal6 + 2w. Difference = (5+3w) - (6+2w) = w -1? Wait internal costs not all equal; but if child internal cost maybe c_child. Greedy has more internal matches (6 vs5) and fewer cross (2 vs3). Since each cross cost w, each internal cost <= w, greedy cost <= alternative. If internal costs = w, equal. So bidirectional not better.

General proof: For any pair matched across children, cost w. For any pair matched within a child, cost <= w. Thus to minimize cost, among all matchings with same total pairs, maximize number of internal pairs. Greedy maximizes internal pairs because it matches min(A_x,B_x) in each child and then as many cross as needed? Wait greedy's total internal pairs = min(A_x,B_x)+min(A_y,B_y) plus? Actually cross pairs are not internal. Total pairs = min(A_u,B_u). Greedy internal pairs = min(A_x,B_x)+min(A_y,B_y). Is that maximum possible internal pairs? Let's see if signs opposite, greedy cross c = min(|S_x|,|S_y|). Total pairs M = (A_u+B_u - |S_x+S_y|)/2. Internal pairs = M - c. Is this equal min(A_x,B_x)+min(A_y,B_y)? We computed yes. This is maximum possible internal pairs because child x can internally match at most min(A_x,B_x), child y at most min(A_y,B_y), independent. So greedy maximizes internal pairs. Since internal cost <= w, and cross cost = w, maximizing internal pairs is optimal (if internal cost could equal w, still no worse). This is a stronger proof. Need ensure greedy indeed maximizes internal pairs: It matches min(A_x,B_x) inside x and min(A_y,B_y) inside y, so maximum. Then remaining surpluses S_x,S_y. To achieve total matching M, it must match some cross pairs. If signs opposite, cross c = min(|S_x|,|S_y|) = (|S_x|+|S_y|-|S_x+S_y|)/2. If same sign, c=0. Is that enough? Yes. Thus greedy cost = optimal child internal costs + c*w. Wait child internal costs: The algorithm processes children recursively, which minimizes cost for matching min(A_child,B_child) pairs within child? Need induction. But if at parent we maximize internal matches in children, we need child algorithm to match exactly min(A_child,B_child) pairs with minimal cost. It does. Good.

But is it always possible for child algorithm to match min(A_child,B_child) pairs while leaving surplus S_child? Yes by induction. So parent can use those matched pairs and surplus.

Thus algorithm correct.

Now, let's think about alternative interpretations. The problem statement: "You are given two sequences of length K: (A_1,...,A_K) and (B_1,...,B_K). It is guaranteed that A_i != B_j (1 <= i,j <= K)." This means for every i,j, A_i != B_j. So no vertex appears in both sequences. But if A_i duplicates, still A_i != B_j for all j, okay. We count net. Good.

"Permute the sequence B freely" means we can reorder B, not change values. If B has duplicates, multiset. Our matching handles duplicates.

Need ensure f(x,y) for x=y? Since A_i != B_j, no pair with same vertex, but if A_i and B_j different vertices, f positive. If duplicates? no same. But f(v,v)=0 would be 0 if allowed; not needed. Our algorithm would handle if leaf had both counts? If A_i = B_j forbidden, but if same vertex appears in A and B in different positions, violates. So no zero-cost matches. If somehow both counts at leaf, bal net would ignore possibility of matching within leaf at cost0. But guarantee prevents. Good.

Let's think about the assignment problem in terms of "minimum cost to balance supplies/demands" on a graph. We have supplies A and demands B at vertices. Cost to ship from supply to demand is minimax edge weight. We need min cost to match supplies to demands (each unit). This is a transportation problem on an ultrametric. The online DSU algorithm is like Kruskal: process edges increasing, whenever two components with opposite net imbalance are connected, ship min imbalance across this edge at cost w. This is analogous to computing Earth Mover Distance on a tree/ultrametric: For each edge of the merge tree, flow across edge? Wait our cost is LCA weight, not sum of edge weights. But in Kruskal tree, when merging components at weight w, matching surplus across components costs w per unit. This is exactly the cost of crossing the cut between the two child components at the moment they merge. In an ultrametric, the distance between any leaf in left and leaf in right is w. So yes.

Could there be a matching where a supply in component A and demand in component B are matched at a lower cost via a path that goes through a third component with edges of lower weight but not directly? If they are in different components before processing weight w, no path with max < w. So no.

Now, let's consider if f(x,y) maybe not exactly ultrametric if graph has multiple edges? It is. Good.

If we later `del data`, those int objects remain because tuple references them. Good.
However, if we do `u = data[idx]`, `v = data[idx+1]`, `w = data[idx+2]`, these are int objects. Good.
But for A/B counts, we don't store A/B ints; after del data, they freed. Good.

Actually `map` over split list; split list of bytes remains until map exhausted? The list is held by list_iterator inside map. It stores bytes objects, memory maybe similar or larger. `list(map(int,...))` creates int list and frees bytes list after expression, but peak includes both. Which is better? Usually `map(int, read().split())` with iterator avoids int list but keeps bytes list; bytes objects memory maybe input size ~? Could be less than int list? Hard. For AtCoder, `list(map(int, read().split()))` is common and accepted for 2e5. We'll use it.

Let's think about if `bal` for vertices should be initialized before reading edges? We need N to allocate. We read N,M,K first. Then allocate `surplus = [0]*(N+1)`. Then read edges, then A/B counts. We can allocate before edges. Good.

Pseudo:
import sys

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    idx = 0
    N = data[idx]; M = data[idx+1]; K = data[idx+2]; idx += 3
    edges = []
    for _ in range(M):
        u = data[idx]; v = data[idx+1]; w = data[idx+2]; idx += 3
        edges.append((w, u, v))
    surplus = [0]*(N+1)
    for _ in range(K):
        v = data[idx]; idx += 1
        surplus[v] += 1
    for _ in range(K):
        v = data[idx]; idx += 1
        surplus[v] -= 1
    del data
    edges.sort(key=lambda x: x[0])  # or default sorts by w then u then v; okay. key maybe faster? default tuple sort compares w then u then v; no issue. key=lambda x:x[0] creates key list? Python sort with key stores keys, memory extra. Default tuple sort no key memory but compares more. For 2e5, either. Use `edges.sort()` because tuple first element w. It will sort by w then u/v; fine.
    parent = list(range(N+1))
    size = [1]*(N+1)
    def find(x):
        while parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = parent[parent[x]]
            x = parent[x]
        return x
    ans = 0
    unions = 0
    for w,u,v in edges:
        ru = find(u); rv = find(v)
        if ru == rv:
            continue
        su = surplus[ru]; sv = surplus[rv]
        if su > 0 and sv < 0:
            if su < -sv:
                ans += w * su
            else:
                ans += w * (-sv)
        elif su < 0 and sv > 0:
            if -su < sv:
                ans += w * (-su)
            else:
                ans += w * sv
        ns = su + sv
        if size[ru] < size[rv]:
            ru, rv = rv, ru
        parent[rv] = ru
        size[ru] += size[rv]
        surplus[ru] = ns
        unions += 1
        if unions == N - 1:
            break
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Need check `surplus[ru] = ns` if we swapped. Suppose original ru had size smaller, rv larger. We swap so ru=rv (larger). ns is sum. Set surplus[ru]=ns. Good. But what about `surplus[rv]` (original ru) after swap? It remains old su. Since rv no longer rep. Good.
But wait: If we swap, we didn't update `surplus[rv]` to maybe 0. Not needed. But if later find on a vertex whose parent chain leads to rv? No, parent[rv] = ru, so find returns ru. Good.

But what if `size[ru] == size[rv]`, no swap, ru remains original ru. We set surplus[ru]=ns. Good.

Algorithm depending order: merge A,B cost1 S+1; merge C,D? C-1,D+1 cost1 S0; merge comps S+1,0 no cost total2. Good.
So the total cost at weight w is w * min(P,N) for each connected component of the graph using edges <=w? Wait, if the final component has total S not zero, it will match some pairs at this weight and leave surplus to higher weights. The number matched at this weight within that component is min(P,N) where P is total positive surplus of lower components, N total negative abs. This is independent of order. Online DSU counts exactly that. Good.

Thus online DSU is correct.

Now, let's think about if we should process edges in ascending weight and maintain surplus; when merging two components, the surpluses `su` and `sv` are net surpluses after all lower-weight matching. The total matched at this weight across all merges equals min(total positive, total negative) among the components at the start of this weight? Actually if multiple merges at same weight, yes.

Wait, the surplus of a component is net after maximizing internal matches at lower weights. Could it be beneficial to not maximize internal matches at lower weights to have both A and B surplus available to match with other components at current weight, reducing current cost? We argued internal lower cost <= current cost, so maximizing internal matches is always optimal. Let's re-examine with a concrete potential counterexample:
At lower weight, component X has two leaves a,b with f(a,b)=1. At current weight w=100, component Y has surplus B. Suppose counts: a A, b B, and X also has extra A? Let's set X has A at a, B at b, A at c (all in X), so X surplus A=1 after matching a-b at cost1. Y has B=1. Total A=2,B=2. Greedy: match a-b cost1, then X surplus A with Y B cost100 total101. Alternative: do not match a-b; match c (A in X) with Y B cost100, and a with b cost1 total101. Same. If alternative matches a with Y B cost100 and c with some outside B at higher cost? Need total B only Y, so c must match b? Actually b is B in X, c A in X, a A. If a-Y cost100, b-c cost1 total101. Same. No improvement.
What if lower internal cost is 50, current 100, alternative a-Y100, b-c50 total150 >101. So greedy better.
What if lower internal cost equals current, tie. So safe.

Now, let's think about if there is a scenario where matching a lower internal pair prevents a surplus of the opposite sign that could be matched at current weight with a component that has same sign surplus? Wait, if X has both A and B, matching them internally reduces both surpluses. If we don't match, X would have one A and one B surplus. At current merge with Y, if Y has A surplus, then X's B could match Y's A at current cost, and X's A would go to ancestor. Greedy: X's A surplus (after internal) matches Y's A? Same sign, no match; both go to ancestor. Which is better? Let's construct:
X has A at a, B at b, and maybe no extra? Then X surplus 0 after internal, not A. Need X has A at a, B at b, A at c => surplus A=1. Y has A surplus=1. Total A=3,B=1, not balanced; need outside B at ancestor. Greedy: match a-b cost low, X surplus c A, Y surplus A, no match at current; at ancestor both A match outside B cost high. Alternative: don't match a-b. Then X has A a, B b, A c; Y has A. At current, match b (B) with Y A cost w, and a,c A go to ancestor. Total: b-Y w + a-outside W + c-outside W. Greedy: a-b low + c-outside W + Y A-outside W. Compare: Alternative - Greedy = (w + aW + cW) - (low + cW + YW) = w - low + (aW - YW). If a and Y both match outside at same ancestor cost W, then difference = w - low >0. Greedy better. If a could match outside at lower than Y? Both A units from same component X/Y? They are in different components maybe ancestor costs differ. But any outside match cost >= w. Y A outside cost >= w. a outside cost >= w. So alternative at least w + W + W, greedy low + W + W' with W'>=w? Actually Y A outside cost >= w, so greedy <= low + W + W'. Alternative = w + W + W'. Since low <= w, greedy <= alternative. Good.
Thus maximizing internal matches safe.

Let's draft final code:

```python
import sys

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    idx = 0
    N = data[idx]
    M = data[idx+1]
    K = data[idx+2]
    idx += 3

    edges = []
    for _ in range(M):
        u = data[idx]
        v = data[idx+1]
        w = data[idx+2]
        idx += 3
        edges.append((w, u, v))

    surplus = [0] * (N + 1)
    for _ in range(K):
        v = data[idx]
        idx += 1
        surplus[v] += 1
    for _ in range(K):
        v = data[idx]
        idx += 1
        surplus[v] -= 1

    del data

    edges.sort()

    parent = list(range(N + 1))
    size = [1] * (N + 1)

    def find(x):
        while parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = parent[parent[x]]
            x = parent[x]
        return x

    ans = 0
    united = 0

    for w, u, v in edges:
        ru = find(u)
        rv = find(v)
        if ru == rv:
            continue

        su = surplus[ru]
        sv = surplus[rv]

        if su > 0 and sv < 0:
            if su < -sv:
                ans += w * su
            else:
                ans += w * (-sv)
        elif su < 0 and sv > 0:
            if -su < sv:
                ans += w * (-su)
            else:
                ans += w * sv

        ns = su + sv

        if size[ru] < size[rv]:
            ru, rv = rv, ru

        parent[rv] = ru
        size[ru] += size[rv]
        surplus[ru] = ns

        united += 1
        if united == N - 1:
            break

    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

Let's double-check the cost addition logic:
If su>0, sv<0, number of pairs = min(su, -sv). Code:
```
if su < -sv: ans += w * su else ans += w * (-sv)
```
Yes.
If su<0, sv>0, number = min(-su, sv). Code:
```
if -su < sv: ans += w * (-su) else ans += w * sv
```
Yes.
If su=0 or sv=0, no cost. If su>0, sv=0, first condition false, second false, no cost. ns=su. Good.
If su<0, sv=0, no cost. Good.
If su>0, sv>0, no cost. ns positive. Good.
If su<0, sv<0, no cost. ns negative. Good.

Now, let's think about if `ns = su + sv` should be computed before or after potential swap? We compute before. Good.
But wait: If we swap `ru, rv` after computing `ns`, and then set `surplus[ru] = ns`, okay. But what about if `size[ru] < size[rv]` and we swap, but `ru` after swap is original rv. The `surplus[ru]` before assignment was `sv`. We overwrite with `ns`. Good. The `surplus[rv]` remains `su`. But if later find on a vertex in original ru's component returns new ru, no issue. However, if later find on original rv's component? original rv is new ru, so its surplus is ns. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with `united == N - 1` break before processing all edges; if graph connected, yes. But what if N=2, after one union break. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with the fact that `edges.sort()` sorts by w then u then v, but if we break early, we don't process all edges; okay.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the input if there are extra spaces/newlines; split handles.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with the code if `K` is large and `surplus` counts create negative values; yes.

Let's maybe consider a random small brute force mentally to ensure online DSU matches assignment:
N=3, edges: 1-2 w10, 2-3 w1. Kruskal: 2-3 w1, 1-2 w10. f(1,2)=10, f(1,3)=10, f(2,3)=1.
A=[1,2], B=[3,3] (disjoint? A 1,2; B3 yes). Optimal: match 1-3 cost10, 2-3 cost1 total11. Algorithm:
surplus1=1,surplus2=1,surplus3=-2.
edge 2-3 w1: su1 sv-2 -> ans1, ns=-1 (comp2,3 surplus -1).
edge 1-2 w10: find1 su1, find2 comp sv-1 -> ans+10=11. Good.
A=[1,3], B=[2,2]: surplus1=1,surplus2=-2,surplus3=1.
edge2-3 w1: su2=-2, sv3=1 -> ans1, ns=-1 (comp2,3 B surplus).
edge1-2 w10: su1=1, comp=-1 -> ans+10=11. Optimal: 3-2 cost1, 1-2 cost10 total11. Good.
A=[1,2], B=[2,3]? Not allowed because A2=2,B1=2. So no.

But note: If we swap, `surplus[ru] = ns` overwrites the surplus of the larger component. Good. But what about `surplus[rv]`? It remains the surplus of the smaller component. If later the smaller component's root is queried? It's not root. Good.

But note: If we swap, `surplus[ru] = ns` overwrites the surplus of the larger component. Good. But what about `surplus[rv]`? It remains the surplus of the smaller component. If later the smaller component's root is queried? It's not root. Good.

But note: If we swap, `surplus[ru] = ns` overwrites the surplus of the larger component. Good. But what about `surplus[rv]`? It remains the surplus of the smaller component. If later the smaller component's root is queried? It's not root. Good.
